\subsection{Spatially correlated idiosyncratic preferences}\label{sec:appendix:nestedlogit}

This appendix section relaxes the assumption that \idiosyncraticphrase s are independent across granular spatial units.
In particular, preferences for residence-workplace pairs have a nested-logit structure,
such that
individual $i$'s indirect utility of residing in $k$ and working in $n$ is
\begin{equation} \label{eqn:nl_utility}
\mathring{U}_{kn}^{i} = \underbrace{\mathring{\epsilon} \ln\left(\frac{w_n}{r_k^\alpha P^{1-\alpha} \delta_{kn}}\right)}_{\equiv \mathring{U}_{kn}} + \mathring{\nu}_{kn}^i
,
\end{equation}
where $\mathring{U}_{kn}$ denotes the mean utility of choice ${kn}$
and
the parameter $\mathring{\epsilon}$ governs the importance of mean utility relative to \idiosyncraticphrase s
in the nested-logit specification.
We allow the \idiosyncraticphrase s $\mathring{\nu}_{kn}^i$ to be correlated across residences within Neighborhood Tabulation Areas (NTAs).
We assume that the vector of \idiosyncraticphrase s has a cumulative distribution
\(
   F(\mathring{\nu}^{i}) = \exp \left\{-\sum_{z} \left(\sum_{(k,n) \in B_z} e^{-\mathring{\nu}_{kn}^i/\zeta}\right)^{\zeta} \right\},
\)
where $B_z$ denotes the set of residence-workplace $kn$ pairs with the residence $k$ in NTA $z$, and
$\zeta$ governs the correlation of \idiosyncraticphrase s
among alternatives in the set $B_z$. 
Whereas draws of idiosyncratic preferences between residence-workplace pairs with residences in distinct NTAs remain uncorrelated,
the within-residence-NTA correlation of idiosyncratic preferences is $1-\zeta^2$.
Given equation~\eqref{eqn:nl_utility} and this distribution of idiosyncratic preferences,
the fraction of people residing in $k$ and working in $n$ is
\begin{equation*}\label{eqn:cont_laborallocation_nestedlogit}
  \mathbb{P}_{kn} 
  =
  \underbrace{
  \frac{
    w_n^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta}(r_k^\alpha \delta_{kn})^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta}
  }{
   \sum_{(k',n') \in B_z}  w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta}(r_{k'}^\alpha \delta_{k'n'})^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta}
  }}_{= \mathbb{P}_{kn|B_z}}
  \times
  \underbrace{
  \frac{%
    \left[ \sum_{(k',n')\in B_z} w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \left( r_{k'}^\alpha \delta_{k'n'} \right)^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \right]^{\zeta}
  }{%
    \sum_{z'} \left[ \sum_{(k',n')\in B_{z'}}  w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \left( r_{k'}^\alpha \delta_{k'n'} \right)^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \right]^{\zeta}
  }
  }_{= \mathbb{P}_{B_z}}
,\end{equation*}
where
$\mathbb{P}_{B_{z}}$ denotes the probability of residing in NTA $z$
and
$\mathbb{P}_{kn|B_z}$ denotes the probability of selecting residence-workplace pair $kn$ among residents of NTA $z$.

Under the assumption of nested-logit preferences and no unobserved disutility ($\delta_{kn}=\bar{\delta}_{kn} \forall kn$),
the counterfactual-baseline ratio of commuters is
\begin{align*}
  \hat{\ell}_{kn} 
  = 
  \left( 
    \frac{
      \hat{w}_n
    }{
      \hat{r}_k^\alpha \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn}
    }
  \right)^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \times
  \frac{%
    \left[ 
      \sum_{(k',n') \in B_z} \left(\frac{\hat{w}_{n'}}{\hat{r}_{k'}^\alpha \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{k'n'}}\right)^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \times \mathbb{P}_{k'n'|B_z} 
    \right]^{\zeta - 1}
  }{%
    \sum_{z'} \mathbb{P}_{B_{z'}}
    \left[ 
      \sum_{(k',n') \in B_{z'}} \left(\frac{\hat{w}_{n'}}{\hat{r}_{k'}^\alpha \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{k'n'}}\right)^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \times \mathbb{P}_{k'n'| B_{z'}} 
    \right]^{\zeta} 
  } \; \text{if} \; \ell_{kn}>0
.\end{align*}


This specification yields the following log likelihood function:
\begin{align*}
    \ln \mathcal{L}
    \equiv
    \sum_{k,n} \ell_{kn}  \ln \mathbb{P}_{kn}
    =
    \sum_{k,n} \ell_{kn}
    \ln
    \left[
       \frac{ 
        w_n^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta}(r_k^\alpha \delta_{kn})^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} 
        \times 
        \left[ \sum_{(k',n')\in B_z} w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \left( r_{k'}^\alpha \delta_{k'n'} \right)^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \right]^{\zeta-1}
        }{
         \sum_{z'} \left[ \sum_{(k',n')\in B_{z'}}  w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \left( r_{k'}^\alpha \delta_{k'n'} \right)^{-\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta} \right]^{\zeta}
        }
    \right].
\end{align*}

We sequentially estimate the parameters of the nested-logit model \citep{Train:2009}.
In the first step, we maximize the log likelihood with respect to
$\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta$, residence fixed effects, workplace fixed effects, and
nest-specific fixed effects.
Given the fixed effects, the estimate of $\mathring{\epsilon}/\zeta$ will be equal to
the estimate of $\epsilon$ in the logit case.
Note that the residence fixed effects are unique only up to a nest-specific normalization.
We normalize the residence fixed effects relative to 
\(r_k^{-\alpha \mathring{\epsilon} / \zeta} \) for some residence $k$ in each NTA $z$.
We index the baseline residence within a NTA as \(\bar{k}(z) \).
Once we impose the normalization, we have residence fixed effects
$\left(\frac{r_k}{r_{\bar{k}(z)}}\right)^{-\alpha\mathring{\epsilon}}$.
We can therefore write
\begin{align*}
	\mathbb{P}_{kn|B_z}
	& = 
	\frac{
		\left[\left(\frac{r_k}{r_{\bar{k}(z)}}\right)^{-\alpha \mathring{\epsilon}} w_n^{\mathring{\epsilon}}\bar{\delta}_{kn}^{-\mathring{\epsilon}}  \right]^{1/\zeta}
	}{
		\sum\limits_{(k',n') \in B_z} \left[\left(\frac{r_{k'}}{r_{\bar{k}(z)}}\right)^{-\alpha \mathring{\epsilon}} w_{n'}^{\mathring{\epsilon}}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}^{-\mathring{\epsilon}}  \right]^{1/\zeta}
	}
	=
	\frac{
		\left[\left(\frac{r_k}{r_{\bar{k}(z)}}\right)^{-\alpha \mathring{\epsilon}} w_n^{\mathring{\epsilon}}\bar{\delta}_{kn}^{-\mathring{\epsilon}}  \right]^{1/\zeta}
	}{
		I_z
    }, \quad \text{ and } \quad
	\mathbb{P}_{B_{z}} 
	& = 
	\frac{
		r_{\bar{k}(z)}^{-\alpha\mathring{\epsilon}} I_z^\zeta
	}{
		\sum_{z'} r_{\bar{k}(z')}^{-\alpha\mathring{\epsilon}} I_{z'}^\zeta
	}
	,\end{align*}
where $I_{z}$ is the inclusive value of nest $z$.
In the second step,
we compute the inclusive values using the estimates from the first step.
Having the estimates of the inclusive values, $\hat{I}_{z}$,
we maximize the following log likelihood function with respect to $r_{\bar{k}(z)}$:
\begin{equation*}
    \ln \tilde{\mathcal{L}}
    \equiv
    \sum_z \left[ \sum_{(k,n)\in B_z} \ell_{kn}\right] \ln \mathbb{P}_{B_{z}}
    = 
    \sum_z 
    \left(
    \left[ \sum_{(k,n)\in B_z} \ell_{kn}\right]
    \times
    \ln 
    \left[
      \frac{
        r_{\bar{k}(z)}^{-\alpha\mathring{\epsilon}} \hat{I}_z^\zeta
      }{
        \sum_{z'} r_{\bar{k}(z')}^{-\alpha\mathring{\epsilon}} \hat{I}_{z'}^\zeta
      }
    \right]
    \right)
\end{equation*}


Note that we cannot simultaneously identify $r_{\bar{k}(z)}$ and parameter $\zeta$ because 
the number of parameters exceeds the number of alternatives. We assume that the value of parameter $\zeta$ is known, in order to estimate $r_{\bar{k}(z)}$ up to a proportionality coefficient.
We compute the equilibrium prices and the remaining economic primitives $\{T_k\}$ and $\{A_n\}$
from the estimated fixed effects and the market-clearing conditions.

We estimate the model for two values of parameter $\zeta$: 0.25 and 0.75.
We estimate each stage of the model by using the
Poisson pseudo maximum likelihood estimator described in Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:estimation}.%
When $\zeta$ is 0.75, 
the elasticity parameter $\mathring{\epsilon}$ is
\input{../input/commuting_elasticity_ntaorigin_0.75.tex}\unskip\
($=7.99 \times \zeta$).
The implied productivities are unchanged,
and the implied land endowments have a correlation of
\input{../input/correlation_landendowment_ntaorigin_0.75.tex}
with the land endowments in the baseline model.
When $\zeta$ is 0.25, 
the elasticity parameter $\mathring{\epsilon}$ is
\input{../input/commuting_elasticity_ntaorigin_0.25.tex}\unskip.
The implied productivities are unchanged,
and the implied land endowments have a correlation of 
\input{../input/correlation_landendowment_ntaorigin_0.25.tex}\unskip\
with the land endowments in the baseline model.

As long as the nested-logit model and the logit-model agree on the unconditional choice probabilities (i.e., they imply the same $\mathbb{P}_{k'n'}$),
we note that in the nested-logit model there is greater within-nest substitution in response to a change in commuting costs than in the simple logit model.
This can be seen in the second of the following three elasticities, noting that $\mathbb{P}_{k'n'\vert B_z} > \mathbb{P}_{k'n'}$:
\newcommand{\ppx}[2]{\ensuremath\frac{\partial #1}{\partial #2}}
\renewcommand{\Pr}{\mathbb{P}}
\begin{align*}
   \ppx{\ln \Pr_{kn}}{\ln \delta_{k'n'}} &= \begin{cases}
            -\epsilon(1 - \Pr_{k'n'\vert B_z}) - \mathring{\epsilon}(\Pr_{k'n'\vert B_z} - \Pr_{k'n'}) & \text{if } k' = k \text{, } n' = n \text{ and } (k,n) \in  B_z \\
            \epsilon \left(  \zeta \Pr_{k'n'} + (1-\zeta)   \Pr_{k'n'\vert B_z}  \right)        & \text{if }  k' \neq k \text{, } (k',n') \in B_z \text{ and } (k,n) \in B_z \\
            \mathring{\epsilon}\Pr_{k'n'}                                                                & \text{if } (k',n') \notin B_z  \text{ and } (k,n) \in B_z 
\end{cases}
\end{align*}

We use the model to compare the predictive performance of the
covariates-based model and the calibrated-shares procedure
in 83 employment booms that are detailed in Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:eventstudies}.
Figure~\ref{fig:event:83events:coefficientdensities:nestedlogit} 
contrasts the predictions of the two procedures for each value of the parameter $\zeta$.
As the figure shows, the results are robust to the 
alternative assumptions about preferences.
Even when idiosyncratic preferences are highly correlated within residence NTAs, 
the covariates-based model considerably outperforms the calibrated-shares procedure.

\subsection{Alternative labor demand elasticities}\label{sec:appendix:sigmavarying}

We compare the predictive performance of the covariates-based model and the calibrated-shares procedure 
for varying values of $\sigma$,
the local labor demand elasticity.
For both $\sigma = 1.1$ and $\sigma = \infty$, the covariates-based model has a lower mean squared error than the calibrated-shares procedure in \input{../input/text_CBM_win.tex} of the \input{../input/text_num_event.tex} events.%
\footnote{Note that the specification with $\sigma=1.1$ does not satisfy the sufficient condition for uniqueness.}
The covariates-based model outperforms the calibrated-shares procedure regardless of the value of $\sigma$ because their predictions are not very sensitive to $\sigma$.
Varying $\sigma$ does not meaningfully change the slope, intercept, or MSEs.
As an example, 
Figure~\ref{fig:appendix:sigmavary:scatter_comparison_slopes} shows that the slope coefficients obtained
when using $\sigma = 1.1$ or $\sigma = \infty$ are very similar to those for $\sigma = 4.0$ in the calibrated-shares procedure.

\subsection{Local increasing returns to scale}\label{sec:appendix:local_inc_returns_es}

This appendix section details the process for computing counterfactual predictions with 
local increasing returns and shows the results of such an exercise.
In short, the predictions from both the covariates-based model and the calibrated-shares procedure
are not sensitive to local increasing returns over the range of relevant values.

Per Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix:local_inc_returns_theory},
we assume that the increasing returns are
external to the firm and that the production function in location $n$ is given by \(q_n = \bar{A}_n L_n^{\eta + 1}\). 
This implies the goods-market-clearing condition is
\begin{equation*}
\bar{A}_n \left( \sum_k \ell_{kn}/\bar{\delta}_{kn}\right)^{1 + \eta} = 
        \frac{w_n^{-\sigma}\bar{A}_n^\sigma\left(\sum_k \ell_{kn} / \bar{\delta}_{kn}\right)^{\eta \sigma}}{P^{1-\sigma}}Y
.\end{equation*}
This can be rearranged to solve for $w_{n}$:
\begin{equation*}
w_n = \bar{A}_n^{\frac{\sigma - 1}{\sigma}} 
            \left(
                \sum_k \ell_{kn}/\bar{\delta}_{kn}
            \right) ^ \frac{\sigma \eta - 1 - \eta}{\sigma} 
            P^{\frac{\sigma - 1}{\sigma}}Y^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}
.\end{equation*}
To express the counterfactual outcomes via exact hat algebra, take ratios to obtain:
\[
    \hat{w}_n = \hat{\bar{A}}_n^{\frac{1}{\eta + 1}}
    \left(\sum_k \hat{y}_{kn} \frac{y_{kn}}{\sum_{k'} y_{k'n}}\right) ^  
          {1 - \frac{\sigma}{(\sigma - 1)(\eta + 1)}}
    \hat{P} ^ \frac{1}{\eta + 1} \hat{Y} ^ \frac{1}{(\sigma - 1)(\eta + 1)}
.\]
This expression for changes in wages generalizes equation~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages} 
to the case of local increasing returns.
Equations~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:rents}~and~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}
are unchanged.
The change in the CES price index 
is
\begin{equation*}
\hat{P} = \left[
  \sum_n 
  \left(\frac{\hat{w}_n}{\hat{\bar{A}}_n \hat{L}^\eta_n}\right)^{1 - \sigma}
  \frac{\sum_k y_{kn}}{Y}
  \right] ^ \frac{1}{1 - \sigma}
.\end{equation*}

\begin{figure}[!h]
    \caption{Event-study slope coefficients by local increasing returns \(\eta\)}
    \label{fig:eta_comparisons:slopes}
    \begin{center}\vspace{-4mm}
    \subfloat[Slope coefficients for $\eta = 0.0028$ versus $\eta = 0$]{
        \includegraphics[width=.49\linewidth]{../input/slope_eta_0.0028_csp_comparison_scatterplot.eps}
    }
    \subfloat[Slope coefficients for $\eta = 0.1$ versus $\eta = 0$]{
        \includegraphics[width=.49\linewidth]{../input/slope_eta_0.1_csp_comparison_scatterplot.eps}
    }\\ \vspace{-2mm}
    \begin{minipage}{0.90\textwidth}{\footnotesize
    \textsc{Notes}:
    The slope coefficients come from regressions of the observed change in commuters on the 
    predicted change in commuters for each of the 83 tract-level employment booms. 
    The baseline case, \(\eta = 0\), is the model without increasing returns.
    \par}\end{minipage}\\ \vspace{4mm}
    \end{center}\vspace{-2em}
\end{figure}

In the event studies of 83 local employment booms, 
the covariates-based model outperforms the calibrated-shares procedure regardless of the value of $\eta$; 
varying $\eta$ does not meaningfully change the slope, intercept, or MSEs.
The differences in predicted commuting flows typically differ only on the order of
\(10^{-2}\) relative to the baseline, even when using values of \(\eta\) larger than 
the sufficient condition for the equilibrium uniqueness.
As an example, 
Figure~\ref{fig:eta_comparisons:slopes} shows that the slope coefficients obtained
when using $\eta = .0028$ or $\eta = 0.1$ are very similar to those for $\eta = 0$ in the calibrated-shares procedure.
\clearpage
\section{Theory: Proofs and extensions}\label{sec:appendix:theory}
\label{sec:appendix:theory:continuum}

Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:continuumexistence} provides a condition on parameter values sufficient for the existence and uniqueness of the continuum model's equilibrium.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:exacthatalgebra} details the exact hat algebra for computing counterfactual outcomes.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:calibratedshares} provides further details on the calibrated-shares procedure.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:continuum:linearchanges} presents a special case in which each counterfactual change is simply proportional to the corresponding baseline share.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:TWFE:slopecoefficient} provides analytical expressions for the slope coefficients in this special case.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:TWFE:baselineerror} derives expected squared errors for baseline shares.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:welfare} derives expressions for welfare changes.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:finite:tradeeqlbmunique} shows that the trade equilibrium of the model with a finite number of individuals is unique.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theorycont} shows that the continuum model is a limiting case of the model with a finite number of individuals.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:uncertainty} analytically characterizes the uncertainty in counterfactual changes caused by individuals' \idiosyncraticphrase s in a two-workplace economy.
The following subsection presents extensions of the bare-bones model in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel} that introduce 
trade costs, the use of land in production, residential amenities, and local increasing returns.



\subsection{Existence and uniqueness of continuum model's equilibrium}\label{sec:appendix:theory:continuumexistence}

We derive a set of parameter values sufficient for the existence and uniqueness of the continuum model's equilibrium.
As shown in Theorem~1 of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023}, the spectral radius of the elasticity matrix characterizes the existence and uniqueness properties of the system.

The equilibrium of the continuum model can be written as
\begin{align}
    \frac{r_k^{1 + \alpha \epsilon} T_k}{\alpha} &= \sum_n D_{kn} w_n^{1+\epsilon} \label{eqn:20201030Crews2:land} \\
    L_n w_n^{-\epsilon} &= \sum_k D_{kn} r_k^{-\alpha \epsilon} \label{eqn:20201030Crews2:labor} \\
    A_n^{1-\sigma} L_n w_n^{\sigma} &= P^{\sigma-1} \sum_{n'} L_{n'} w_{n'}, \label{eqn:20201030Crews2:goods}
\end{align}
where, for notational convenience, we define
\begin{align*}
\Theta &\equiv \sum_{k}\sum_{n} w_{n}^{\epsilon} (r_k^{\alpha} \delta_{kn})^{-\epsilon}
\\
D_{kn} &\equiv L \Theta^{-1} \delta_{kn}^{-\epsilon} \bar{\delta}_{kn}^{-1}.
\end{align*}

Equations~\eqref{eqn:20201030Crews2:land}--\eqref{eqn:20201030Crews2:goods}
are $3 \times N$ equations in $3 \times N$ unknowns $\{r_n, w_n, L_n\}_{n=1,2,\ldots,N}$
given values for $\Theta$ and $P$.
Although $\Theta$ and $P$ are endogenous,
they do not vary across locations.
Accordingly, they fall under the class of endogenous scalars
discussed in Remark~4 of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023},
which leave the conclusion of their Theorem~1 Part ii.b unchanged.
 
Following Remark~5 of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023},
we construct the intermediate matrices $\mathbf{\Gamma}$ and $\mathbf{B}$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{\Gamma} = \begin{bmatrix}
        1 + \alpha \epsilon & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & -\epsilon & 1 \\
        0 & \sigma & 1 \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \qquad
    \mathbf{B} = \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 1+\epsilon & 0 \\
        -\alpha \epsilon & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & 1 & 1 \\
    \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Then we can construct the final matrix $\mathbf{A}$ defined by $(\mathbf{A})_{hh'} \equiv \left|\left(\mathbf{B} \mathbf{\Gamma}^{-1}\right)_{hh'}\right|$:
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{B} \mathbf{\Gamma}^{-1}
    &=
    \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & 1+\epsilon & 0 \\
        -\alpha \epsilon & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & 1 & 1 \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \frac{1}{1 + \alpha \epsilon} & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & -\frac{1}{\sigma + \epsilon} & \frac{1}{\sigma + \epsilon} \\
        0 & \frac{\sigma}{\sigma + \epsilon} & \frac{\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon} \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    , \qquad
    \mathbf{A} =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon} & \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon} \\
        \frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1 + \alpha \epsilon} & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & \frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma + \epsilon} & \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon} \\
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{align*}
where $(\mathbf{A})_{hh'}$ denotes the $(h,h')^{\text{th}}$ entry of the matrix $\mathbf{A}$,
which is the absolute value of the $(h,h')^{\text{th}}$ entry of the matrix $\mathbf{B} \mathbf{\Gamma}^{-1}$.
We assume $\sigma>1$.

We want to show that the spectral radius,
the absolute value of the maximum eigenvalue,
of $\mathbf{A}$ (denoted $\rho(\mathbf{A})$) equals one.
The reasoning in Remark 6 in Appendix~B.1.5 of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023} demonstrates that $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \geq 1$.
Thus, it remains to show that $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \leq 1$.
To do so, we will use Lemma~1 in Appendix B.1.6 from \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023}.
Consider the matrix
\begin{equation*}
    \lambda I - \mathbf{A} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \lambda & -\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma+\epsilon} & -\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma+\epsilon} \\
        -\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon} & \lambda & 0 \\
        0 & -\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma + \epsilon} & \lambda - \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon} \\
    \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Define the function $f(\lambda) \equiv \text{det}(\lambda I - \mathbf{A})$,
and denote its $k$th derivative by $f_k(\lambda)$:
\begin{align*}
f(\lambda) &= \lambda^3 - \lambda^2 \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) 
- \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right) \left(\lambda - \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma+\epsilon}\right)
- \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma+\epsilon}\right) \\
f_1(\lambda) &= 3 \lambda^2 - 2 \lambda \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) - \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right)\\
f_2(\lambda) &= 6 \lambda - \frac{2 (1+\epsilon)}{\sigma + \epsilon}.
\end{align*}
Lemma~1 of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023} states that $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \leq 1$ if and only if $f_k(1) \geq 0$ for $k=0,1,2$.
\begin{align*}
    f(1) &= 1 - \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma+\epsilon}
    - \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right)
    + \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{2 + \epsilon - \sigma}{\sigma+\epsilon}\right) \\
    f_1(1) &= 3 - 2 \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) - \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right) \\
    f_2(1) &= 6 - \frac{2 (1+\epsilon)}{\sigma + \epsilon}.
\end{align*}
Both $f_1(1) \geq 0$ and $f_2(1) \geq 0$ for any $\sigma>1$ and $\alpha, \epsilon > 0$.
Inequality $f(1) \geq 0$  can be restated as
   
   
   
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn:20201030Crews2:paramcond}
    \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1+\alpha \epsilon}\right) \leq \frac{1}{2}.
\end{equation}
Note that condition~\eqref{eqn:20201030Crews2:paramcond}
is satisfied by our baseline parameter values
($\alpha = 0.24$, $\sigma = 4$, $\epsilon = 7.986$).
Under condition~\eqref{eqn:20201030Crews2:paramcond}, $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \leq 1$,
which means that $\rho(\mathbf{A}) = 1$.
Thus, by Theorem~1 Part ii.b of \cite{AllenArkolakisLi:2023},
if condition~\eqref{eqn:20201030Crews2:paramcond} is true,
the equilibrium exists and is unique up to scale.
\subsection{Exact hat algebra}\label{sec:appendix:theory:exacthatalgebra}

This section shows how to express a counterfactual equilibrium of the model in terms of
counterfactual endogenous outcomes relative to baseline endogenous outcomes,
counterfactual exogenous parameters relative to baseline exogenous parameters,
constant elasticities, 
and
baseline equilibrium shares.
This method has been dubbed ``exact hat algebra'' in the international trade literature.


One can solve for the counterfactual equilibrium variables associated with
combinations of counterfactual-to-baseline ratios of productivities $\hat{A}_{n}$, land endowments $\hat{T}_{k}$, and commuting costs $\hat{ \lambda}_{kn}$ and $\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} $.

The derivation of $\hat{w}_n$ starts from equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing}:
\begin{equation*}
	A_n \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}} 
=
\frac{\left({w_n}/{A_n}\right)^{-\sigma}}{P^{1-\sigma}} Y \qquad \forall n
.
\end{equation*}
Rearranging terms to isolate $w_n$ and then taking ratios yields
\begin{align} \label{eqn:hat_wage}
\hat{w}_n
=
\hat{A}_{n}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} 
\underbrace{\left(\frac{\sum_k \frac{\ell'_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}'_{kn}}}{\sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}} \right)^\frac{-1}{\sigma} }_{\equiv \hat{L}_{n}^\frac{-1}{\sigma}}
\underbrace{\left(\frac{P'}{P}\right)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} }_{\equiv \hat{P}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}} 
\underbrace{\left(\frac{\sum_{k',n'} y'_{k'n'}}{\sum_{k',n'} y_{k'n'}} \right)^\frac{1}{\sigma}}_{\equiv \hat{Y}^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}}. 
\end{align}
Next, we rewrite this in terms of $\frac{\hat{\ell}_{kn}}{\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn}}$ and baseline shares:
\begin{align*}
	\hat{w}_{n}
&= \hat{A}_{n}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\left(\frac{\sum_k \frac{\hat{\ell}_{kn}}{\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn}} \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}}{\sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}} \right)^\frac{-1}{\sigma}
\hat{P}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\hat{Y}^{\frac{1}{\sigma}} \\
&= \hat{A}_{n}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\left(\sum_k \frac{\hat{\ell}_{kn}}{\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn}} \frac{\frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}}{\sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}} \right)^\frac{-1}{\sigma}
\hat{P}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\hat{Y}^{\frac{1}{\sigma}} 
\end{align*}
Multiply both sides by $\hat{w}_{n}^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}$, simplify, and take both sides to the power $\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}$ to obtain
\begin{align*} \label{eqn:hat_wage_expression} 
\hat{w}_{n}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
&= \hat{A}_{n}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\left(\sum_{k}\hat{y}_{kn}\frac{y_{kn}}{\sum_{k'}y_{k'n}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\sigma}}
\hat{P}^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} 
\hat{Y}^{\frac{1}{\sigma}} \nonumber \\
\hat{w}_{n} &= \hat{A}_{n}\left(\sum_{k}\hat{y}_{kn}\frac{y_{kn}}{\sum_{k'}y_{k'n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\sigma}} 
\hat{P}
\hat{Y}^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}}
,\end{align*}
which is equation~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages}.

To derive $\hat{P}$,
rearrange terms and employ goods market clearing:
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{P}^{1-\sigma} 
	=
	\sum_n \frac{\left( \frac{w_n'}{A_n'} \right)^{1-\sigma}}{\sum_{n'} \left( \frac{w_{n'}}{A_{n'}} \right)^{1-\sigma}}
	=
	\sum_n \left( \frac{\hat{w}_n}{\hat{A}_n} \right)^{1-\sigma} \frac{\left( \frac{w_n}{A_n} \right)^{1-\sigma}}{\sum_{n'} \left( \frac{w_{n'}}{A_{n'}} \right)^{1-\sigma}}
	=
	\sum_n \left( \frac{\hat{w}_n}{\hat{A}_n} \right)^{1-\sigma} \sum_k \frac{y_{kn}}{Y}
.\end{equation*}
This implies
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{P} = \left( \sum_n \left( \frac{\hat{w}_n}{\hat{A}_n} \right)^{1-\sigma} \sum_k \frac{y_{kn}}{Y} \right)^{\frac{1}{1-\sigma}}.
\end{equation*}
This shows that relative goods price index $\hat{P}$ can be written in terms of relative endogenous wages, relative exogenous productivities, and baseline (earnings) shares.

The ratio of counterfactual nominal output $Y'$ to baseline nominal output $Y$ is
\begin{equation*}
	\hat{Y}
	=
	\frac{Y'}{Y}
	=
	\frac{\sum_{k,n} y'_{kn}}{ \sum_{k,n} y_{kn}}
	=
	\frac{\sum_{k,n} \hat{y}_{kn} y_{kn}}{\sum_{k,n} y_{kn}}
	=
	\sum_{k,n} \hat{y}_{kn} \frac{y_{kn}}{Y}
\end{equation*}

With the changes in output, changes in prices, and changes in wages,
equation~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages} is expressed entirely in terms of hats and shares.

Next, we derive $\hat{r}_k$.
Dividing the counterfactual version of equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing} by the baseline version yields
\begin{equation*} \label{eqn:hat_rent_expression}
\hat{T}_k = \hat{r}_k^{-1} \frac{\sum_n y'_{kn}}{\sum_n y_{kn}}
.\end{equation*}
Rearranging this expression yields
\begin{equation*}
\hat{r}_k = \hat{T}_k^{-1} \frac{\sum_n y'_{kn}}{\sum_n y_{kn}} = \hat{T}_k^{-1} \frac{\sum_n \hat{y}_{kn} y_{kn}}{\sum_n y_{kn}} = \hat{T}_{k}^{-1} \sum_{n} \hat{y}_{kn} \frac{y_{kn}}{\sum_{n'} y_{kn'}} \nonumber
,\end{equation*}
which is equation~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:rents}. 


Finally, taking the ratio of counterfactual $\ell'_{kn}$ to baseline $\ell_{kn}$ using equation~\eqref{eqn:cont_laborallocation} yields, after considerable manipulation, equation~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}:
\begin{align*}
\hat{\ell}_{kn}
&= \frac{\frac{w_n^{'\epsilon}(r_k^{'\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{kn}^{'}\lambda_{kn}^{'})^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'}w_{n'}^{'\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{'\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}^{'}\lambda_{k'n'}^{'})^{-\epsilon}}}{\frac{w_n^{\epsilon}(r_k^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{kn}\lambda_{kn})^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'}w_{n'}^{\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}\lambda_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}} \\
&= \frac{\hat{w}_{n}^{\epsilon} (\hat{r}_{k}^{\alpha} \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} \hat{\lambda}_{kn} )^{-\epsilon}}{\frac{\sum_{k',n'}w_{n'}^{'\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{'\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}^{'}\lambda_{k'n'}^{'})^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'}w_{n'}^{\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}\lambda_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}} \\
&= \frac{\hat{w}_{n}^{\epsilon} (\hat{r}_{k}^{\alpha} \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} \hat{\lambda}_{kn} )^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'}\frac{w_{n'}^{'\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{'\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}^{'}\lambda_{k'n'}^{'})^{-\epsilon}}{w_{n'}^{\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}\lambda_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}\frac{w_{n'}^{\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}\lambda_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'}w_{n'}^{\epsilon}(r_{k'}^{\alpha}\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}\lambda_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}} \\
&= \frac{\hat{w}_{n}^{\epsilon} (\hat{r}_{k}^{\alpha} \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} \hat{\lambda}_{kn} )^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'} \hat{w}_{n'}^{\epsilon} (\hat{r}_{k'}^{\alpha} \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{k'n'} \hat{\lambda}_{k'n'} )^{-\epsilon} \frac{\ell_{k'n'}}{L}}.
\end{align*}

Together, equations~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages}, \eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:rents}, and~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}
deliver $\hat{w}_n$, $\hat{r}_k$, and $\hat{\ell}_{kn}$ 
given the elasticities $\sigma$, $\alpha$, and $\epsilon$, commuting costs $\bar{\delta}_{kn}$, baseline equilibrium values $\ell_{kn}$ and $w_{n}$, and
counterfactual-to-baseline ratios $\hat{A}_{n}$, $\hat{T}_{k}$, $\hat{\lambda}_{kn}$, and $\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} $.

\subsubsection{An example of baseline shares (and elasticities) as sufficient statistics\label{appendix:sec:EHA:suffstats}}

Suppose that each residential location has an exogenous amenity level $B_k$ such that 
$i$'s indirect utility from residing in $k$ and working in $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
U_{kn}^{i} = \epsilon \ln\left(\frac{B_k w_n}{r_k^\alpha P^{1-\alpha} \delta_{kn}}\right) + \nu_{kn}^{i}
.
\end{equation*}
In the model presented in the main text,
residential amenities are do not vary across locations: 
$B_{k} = 1 \ \forall k$.
The probability of choosing residential-workplace pair $kn$ is now
\begin{equation*}
	\Pr(U_{kn}^{i} > U_{k'n'}^{i} \ \forall (k',n') \neq (k,n)) 
	=
	\frac
	{w_{n}^\epsilon \left(B_{k}^{-1}r_{k}^\alpha  \delta_{kn}\right)^{-\epsilon}}
	{\sum_{k',n'} w_{n'}^\epsilon \left(B_{k'}^{-1}r_{k'}^\alpha  \delta_{k'n'}\right)^{-\epsilon}}
	.
\end{equation*}
A residence is more attractive when it has higher amenities or greater land supply (lower rents).

Separating these amenities and land supplies is unnecessary for some counterfactual analysis.
Infinitely many combinations of amenity and land-supply parameters could deliver the same baseline shares,
but one does not need to identify all the model parameters in levels in order to compute counterfactual changes.
Suppose that residential amenities vary across locations but are unchanged in the counterfactual scenario:
$\hat{B}_{k} = 1 \ \forall k$.
Following the same steps as above,
one can derive a system of equations characterizing the counterfactual equilibrium
that is identical to equations~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages}--\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}.
Given the baseline shares and elasticities (the sufficient statistics),
counterfactual changes do not depend on distinguishing between amenities $B_k$ and land supply $T_k$.

\subsection{Calibrated-shares procedure}\label{sec:appendix:theory:calibratedshares}

The calibrated-shares procedure uses the observed $\ell_{kn}$ and $y_{kn} = w_n \ell_{kn} / \bar{\delta}_{kn}$ in equations~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages}--\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}.
This implicitly calibrates combinations of model parameters.
For example,
this procedure implicitly rationalizes zero-commuters observations with infinite commuting costs, $\ell_{kn}=0 \iff \delta_{kn} = \infty$.
This procedure cannot characterize cases in which $\ell_{kn}=0$ and $\ell'_{kn} \neq 0$
because the object $\hat{\delta}_{kn}^{-\epsilon} = \left(\frac{\delta_{kn}}{\delta'_{kn}}\right)^{\epsilon}$ is not sensibly defined if $\delta_{kn} = \infty$ and $\delta'_{kn} < \infty$.

This procedure does not identify the parameters $A_n$, $T_k$, and $\delta_{kn}$.
Given the elasticities $\sigma$, $\alpha$, and $\epsilon$ and the baseline equilibrium shares $\ell_{kn}$ and $y_{kn}$,
equations~\eqref{eqn:cont_laborallocation},
\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing}, and
\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing}
are insufficient to separately identify $T_k$ and $\delta_{kn}$.
One would also need to observe land prices $r_k$.

When implementing this procedure in Sections~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:eventstudies} and~\ref{sec:AmazonHQ2:firstmoments},
we compute tract-level workplace wages using LODES and ZIP Business Patterns data following \cite{OwensRossi-HansbergSarte:2020}.

\subsection{Special case relating counterfactual errors to baseline-share errors\label{sec:appendix:theory:continuum:linearchanges}}

In the continuum model with exogenous residential amenities (see Appendix~\ref{appendix:sec:EHA:suffstats}), no housing consumption ($\alpha = 0$), and perfectly elastic labor demand ($\sigma = \infty$),
there is a linear mapping from baseline shares to predicted changes caused by a productivity change.

If individuals do not consume housing, $\alpha = 0$, the model simplifies.
The endogenous variable $r_k$ and the land-market-clearing condition are no longer part of the model.
(For the counterfactual outcomes of interest, this is isomorphic to land being perfectly elastically supplied at a fixed rent $r_k$.)
The price index is $P$ in all residential locations.
The labor-market-clearing condition is unchanged.
The labor-allocation equation is now
\begin{equation*}
  \Pr(U_{kn}^{i} > U_{k'n'}^{i} \ \forall (k',n') \neq (k,n)) 
  =
  \frac
  {\left(B_k {w}_{n}\right)^\epsilon \delta_{kn}^{-\epsilon}}
  {\sum_{k',n'} \left(B_{k'} {w}_{n'}\right)^\epsilon \delta_{k'n'}^{-\epsilon}}
  .
\end{equation*}
Given this, the PPML estimator's residential and workplace fixed effects are proportionate to
$\epsilon \ln B_{k}$ and $\epsilon \ln {w}_{n}$, respectively.

For this special case with perfectly elastic labor demand,
the system of two equations defining counterfactual changes is
\begin{equation*}
\hat{w}_{n}
=
\hat{A}_n
\qquad \text{ and } \qquad
\hat{\ell}_{kn} 
=
\frac{\hat{w}_{n}^{\epsilon} \hat{B}_{k}^{\epsilon} \left(\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn}\hat{ \lambda}_{kn} \right)^{-\epsilon}}{\sum_{k',n'} \hat{w}_{n'}^{\epsilon} \hat{B}_{k'}^{\epsilon} \left( \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{k'n'}\hat{ \lambda}_{k'n'} \right)^{-\epsilon} \frac{\ell_{k'n'}}{L}} \qquad \text{ if } \ell_{kn} > 0
.\end{equation*}
If workplace $n^{*}$ alone has a productivity increase ($\hat{A}_{n^{*}} > 1, \hat{A}_{n} = 1 \ \forall n \neq n^{*}$)
and there are no changes to amenities nor commuting costs ($\hat{B}_{k} =1 \ \forall k $, $\hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} = \hat{\lambda}_{kn} =1 \ \forall kn $),
then $\hat{\ell}_{kn^{*}}$ is constant across $k$ and changes in commuters to workplace $n^{*}$ vary only with baseline shares:
\begin{align*}
\hat{\ell}_{kn^{*}} 
&=
\frac{\hat{A}_{n^{*}}^{\epsilon}}{\hat{A}_{n^{*}}^{\epsilon} \frac{\sum_{k'}\ell_{k'n^{*}}}{L} + \frac{L - \sum_{k'}\ell_{k'n^{*}}}{L}}
=
\hat{L}_{n^{*}}
\ \forall k
\\
\Delta {\ell}_{kn^{*}} 
&=
\left(\hat{\ell}_{kn^{*}}  - 1 \right){\ell}_{kn^{*}}
=
\left(\hat{L}_{n^{*}}  - 1 \right){\ell}_{kn^{*}}
.\end{align*}
Because the counterfactual change $\Delta {\ell}_{kn^{*}}$ is proportional to the baseline employment ${\ell}_{kn^{*}}$ in this simple case,
if the baseline share ${\ell}_{kn^{*}}/L$ contains estimation error,
a counterfactual change computed using that share will inherit that error proportionally.

The change in the number of workers employed at workplace $n^{*}$ in this simple example is
$$
\sum_{k} \Delta {\ell}_{kn^{*}} 
=
\left(\hat{L}_{n^{*}}  - 1 \right) \sum_{k} \ell_{kn^{*}}
.$$
If the estimation error in $\ell_{kn^{*}}$ is sampling error,
workplace-level employment will be less noisy than a single residence-workplace pair:
the coefficient of variation for a binomial distribution with $I$ draws and probability $p$ is $\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p I}}$, which is decreasing in $p$,
and
the probability of choosing workplace $n$
is greater than the probability of choosing residence-workplace pair $kn$ ($p_n = \sum_{k} p_{kn} \geq p_{kn}$).

By contrast, changes in residents caused by a productivity shock are sensitive to pair-specific linkages.
The change in the number of residents at residence $k^{*}$ in this simple example is
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n} \Delta {\ell}_{k^{*}n}
=
\frac{\hat{L}_{n^{*}}  - 1}{L - \sum_{k}\ell_{kn^{*}}} \sum_{n} \ell_{k^{*}n}
\left[\frac{\ell_{k^{*} n^{*}}}{\sum_{n} \ell_{k^{*}n}} L  - \sum_{k}\ell_{kn^{*}} \right]
.\end{equation*}
This expression says that the change in residents is governed by the difference (in brackets)
between a gross increase in residents,
which is proportional to  the share of residents who work at the treated workplace $n^{*}$ in the baseline equilibrium,
and a gross decrease in residents that is common across residential locations.

\subsection{Slope coefficients from regressing true probabilities on estimated shares}\label{sec:appendix:theory:TWFE:slopecoefficient}

This section discusses the slope coefficients for the calibrated-shares procedure and the covariates-based approach.
In the special case ($\alpha = 0$, $\sigma = \infty$, and exogenous residential amenities),
regressing the true changes in outcomes on the predicted changes is equivalent to regressing the true baseline shares on the estimated baseline shares (see Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:continuum:linearchanges}).
Thus, the object of interest is the slope coefficient $\beta$ from regressing the true probability $p_{kn}$ on the estimated probability $\hat{p}_{kn}$ in a given finite realization,
where the true probability from the continuum model $p_{kn} \equiv \Pr(U_{kn}^{i} > U_{k'n'}^{i} \ \forall (k',n') \neq (k,n))$ is the right side of equation~\eqref{eqn:cont_laborallocation}.
Define the error $e_{kn} \equiv p_{kn} - \hat{p}_{kn}$.
We denote the variance in errors across elements of the choice set (across residence-workplace pairs indexed by $kn$) within a finite realization by
$$
\widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})
\equiv
\frac{1}{KN} \sum_{k,n} e_{kn}^{2}
.$$
This definition exploits the fact that $\sum_{k,n} e_{kn} = 0$ in any finite realization.
Thus, $\widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})$ is the mean squared error (MSE).
Similarly, denote the covariance of $p_{kn}$ and $e_{kn}$ across elements of the choice set within a finite realization by
$
\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})
\equiv
\frac{1}{KN} \sum_{k,n} p_{kn} e_{kn}
$
and the covariance of $p_{kn}$ and $\hat{p}_{kn}$ by
$
\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},\hat{p}_{kn})
\equiv
\frac{1}{KN} \sum_{k,n} p_{kn} \hat{p}_{kn}
-
\frac{1}{K^2 N^2} \sum_{k,n} p_{kn} \sum_{k,n} \hat{p}_{kn}
$
.
Finally, the variance of $p_{kn}$ across $kn$ pairs within a realization is 
$\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn}) \equiv \frac{1}{KN} \sum_{k,n} \left(p_{kn} - \frac{1}{KN}\right)^2$,
where we exploit the fact that the average $p_{kn}$ must be $\frac{1}{KN}$ because $\sum_{k,n} p_{kn} = 1$.
Note that $p_{kn}$ is a fixed feature of the data-generating process that does not vary across realizations,
so $\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn}) = \text{Var}(p_{kn})$.

The slope coefficient of interest is
\begin{equation*}
\hat{\beta}
=
\frac{\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},\hat{p}_{kn})}{\widehat{\text{Var}}(\hat{p}_{kn})}
=
\frac{\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},p_{kn} - e_{kn})}{\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn} - e_{kn})}
=
\frac{\text{Var}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})}{\text{Var}(p_{kn}) + \widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn}) - 2\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})}
.\end{equation*}

First, consider the calibrated-shares procedure,
which uses $\hat{p}_{kn} = s_{kn}$, the realized share in the observed sample.
Because the calibrated-shares procedure is the frequency estimator that simply uses the observed shares as its estimate of the true probability,
$\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) \approx 0$ (this is true in expectation and typically close to zero in our simulations of finite realizations).%
\footnote{
	The population expectation of $\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) \propto \sum_{kn} p_{kn} e_{kn} - \sum_{kn}p_{kn} \sum_{kn}e_{kn} = \sum_{kn} p_{kn} e_{kn}$ over realizations is zero for the calibrated-shares procedure,
	because $\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{kn} p_{kn} e_{kn}\right) = \sum_{kn} p_{kn} \mathbb{E}\left(e_{kn}\right) = 0$ given that $\mathbb{E}(s_{kn}) = p_{kn}$.
}
If the errors are uncorrelated with the true probabilities, 
$\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) = 0$,
then the slope coefficient is
$\frac{\text{Var}(p_{kn})}{\text{Var}(p_{kn}) + \text{MSE}}$.
Given the data-generating process (given $\text{Var}(p_{kn})$) and orthogonal errors,
the attenuation of the slope coefficient is increasing in the mean squared error.
That is, here the calibrated-shares procedure's finite-sample errors manifest as both a lower slope coefficient and a higher mean squared error.

Second, consider the two-way fixed-effects estimator as an example of a covariates-based specification.
Suppose that the data-generating process is $p_{kn} = p_k p_n \lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon}$
with $\sum_n p_n \lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} = 1$ and $\sum_k p_k \lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} = 1$.
The probabilities of choosing residence $k$ and of choosing workplace $n$ are
$p_k$ and $p_n$, respectively.%
\footnote{
    This requires
    $\sum_n p_n \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} = 1 \ \forall k$
    so that $p_k = \sum_{n} p_{kn} \ \forall k$
    and similarly
    $\sum_{k} p_k \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} = 1 \ \forall n$
    so that $p_n = \sum_{k} p_{kn} \ \forall n$.
    These normalize $\lambda_{kn}$ without loss of generality:
    common $k$- and $n$-level components (``fixed effects'') are in $p_k$ and $p_n$, not $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}$.
}
The two-way fixed-effects estimator is $\hat{p}_{kn} = s_k s_n$,
where $s_k = \sum_n s_{kn}$ and $s_n = \sum_k s_{kn}$.
The two-way fixed-effects estimator misses the contribution of $\lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon}$ to the true probability $p_{kn}$,
so typically $\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) > 0$.
This need not attenuate the slope coefficient, however.
Intuitively, the estimator's omission of the (orthogonal) unobserved commuting cost reduces the $R^2$ of the regression, not the slope coefficient.
Note that the slope coefficient can be written as
\begin{equation*}
\hat{\beta}
=
\frac{\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})}{\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) - \left(\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})\right)}
.\end{equation*}
\noindent If the numerator $\text{Var}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})$ is much larger than $\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})$,
then the slope coefficient is close to one.
Consider the case in which the fixed effects are reliably estimated (assume $s_{k} \approx p_k$, $s_n \approx p_n$, and $s_k s_n \approx p_k p_n$).
(If $s_k$ and $s_n$ are not reliable estimators of $p_k$ and $p_n$, then the frequency estimator $s_{kn}$ will estimate $p_{kn}$ very poorly.)
In this case, the two differences of interest are approximately
\begin{align*}
\text{Var}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})
&\approx
\frac{1}{KN} \sum_{kn} p_k^2 p_n^2\lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} - \frac{1}{K^2 N^2}
\\
\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})
&\approx
\frac{1}{KN} \sum_{kn} [p_k p_n]^2 (\lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} - 1)
.\end{align*}
Therefore, the numerator is much larger ($\gg$) than the other difference if
\begin{align*}
\text{Var}(p_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})
&\gg
\left[\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn}) - \widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})\right]
\\
\iff
0
&\ll
K^2 N^2 \text{Var}(p_{k}) \text{Var}(p_{n}) + K^2 \text{Var}(p_{k}) + N^2 \text{Var}(p_{n}) 
\end{align*}
This implies that the slope coefficient for the two-way fixed-effects estimator will be close to one so long as there is substantial variation in the residence and employment shares
($\text{Var}(p_{k}) \gg 0, \text{Var}(p_{n}) \gg 0$).

If one ran this regression for a subset of location pairs, such as a single workplace $n^*$,
the same expression for the slope coefficient would apply.
Note that in this case, the sum of the prediction errors for a single workplace $n^*$, $\sum_{k} e_{kn^*}$, need not equal zero,
so that $\widehat{\text{Var}}(e_{kn})$ equals the MSE plus the mean prediction error squared.
Similarly, the definitions of $\widehat{\text{Cov}}(p_{kn},e_{kn})$ and $\widehat{\text{Var}}(p_{kn})$
must be extended to allow for means not equal to zero and $\frac{1}{KN}$, respectively.
In the Monte Carlo simulations of Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:MonteCarlo} and event studies of Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:eventstudies},
we match the change in total employment in the workplace of interest,
so that by construction the sum of the prediction errors is indeed zero.

\subsection{Simple example of expected squared error for baseline shares}\label{sec:appendix:theory:TWFE:baselineerror}

This section contrasts the expected squared errors for baseline shares produced by the calibrated-shares procedure and the covariates-based approach.
Researchers face a familiar trade-off: a more flexible model with more parameters requires more data to be estimated precisely.
The calibrated-shares procedure estimates a saturated model with an arbitrary commuting cost for each residence-workplace pair,
while the covariates-based specification assumes that bilateral commuting costs depend on few observables.
Their relative performance depends on how many commuters are observed per residence-workplace pair and the magnitudes of the unobserved commuting costs.

Consider an arbitrary data-generating process with true probabilities
$p_{kn} \equiv \Pr(U_{kn}^{i} > U_{k'n'}^{i} \ \forall (k',n') \neq (k,n))$.
The expected squared error for a baseline share $\frac{\ell_{kn}}{L}$ produced by an estimator $\hat{p}_{kn}$
is $\mathbb{E}\left(p_{kn} - \hat{p}_{kn}\right)^2$.
The calibrated-shares procedure uses the frequency estimator $\hat{p}_{kn} = s_{kn}$, where $s_{kn}$ is the realized share in the observed sample.
The covariates-based approach imposes more structure.
Consider a simple example in which the data-generating process is 
$p_{kn}
= p_k p_n \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}$,
so bilateral commuting costs are entirely unobserved.
The probabilities of choosing residence $k$ and of choosing workplace $n$ are
$p_k$ and $p_n$, respectively.
The covariates-based specification for this example
is the two-way-fixed-effects estimator:
$\hat{p}_{kn} = s_k s_n$,
where $s_k$ is the share of residence $k$ and $s_n$ is the share of workplace $n$.

The calibrated-shares procedure is an unbiased estimator ($\mathbb{E}(s_{kn}) = p_{kn}$),
so its expected squared error for baseline share $\frac{\ell_{kn}}{L}$ is its finite-sample variance.
With $I$ observed commuters, this is
$
\mathbb{E}\left(p_{kn} - {s}_{kn}\right)^2
=
\text{var}\left(s_{kn}\right)
=
\frac{1}{I} p_{kn}(1-p_{kn})
$.
This variance goes to zero as the number of observations goes to infinity,
$\lim_{I \to \infty}\text{var}\left(s_{kn}\right) = 0$,
so the only shortcoming of the calibrated-shares procedure here is its finite-sample performance.

By imposing more structure,
the covariates-based approach delivers
an estimator that typically has much lower variance but is biased to the extent that it omits unobserved commuting costs.
In the simple example,
because the estimator $\hat{p}_{kn} = s_k s_n$ uses 
residence- and workplace-level shares rather than the share of a single residence-workplace pair $s_{kn}$,
the covariates-based specification has a much smaller variance in granular settings with finite individuals.%
\footnote{\label{footnote:variance:magnitudes}%
Given thousands of residences and workplaces, $\mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)$ is two or three orders of magnitude smaller than $\mathrm{var}(s_{kn})$.
It can be shown that
$
\mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)
=
\frac{1}{I} p_k p_n (1-p_k) (1-p_n)
\left(\frac{1}{I} + \frac{p_n}{1-p_n} + \frac{p_k}{1-p_k}\right)
+
\frac{1}{I^3} p_k p_n \left(\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}-1\right)
\left[1 + 2(I-1)(p_k + p_n) + \left((I-2) (2I + \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} - 3) -2I \right) p_k p_n \right]
$.
For example, given the NYC setting with $I \approx 2.5 \times 10^6$ and $p_k \approx p_n \approx 0.5 \times 10^{-3}$,
$\mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)$ is on the order of $10^{-16}$
and
$\mathrm{var}(s_{kn})$ is on the order of $10^{-13}$.
}
Unless $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} = 1$,
the two-way-fixed-effects estimator is biased because $\mathbb{E}(s_{k}s_{n}) \neq p_{kn}$.
Its expected squared error is
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb{E}\left[(p_{kn} - \hat{p}_{kn})^2\right] 
= \left[\mathbb{E}(p_{kn} - s_k s_n)\right]^2 + \mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)
= \left[\frac{I-1}{I} p_k p_n (1- \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn})\right]^2 + \mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)
,\end{equation*}
where the first term is the square of the bias of the estimator and the second term is its sample variance.%
\footnote{Note that $\hat{p}_{kn} = s_k s_n$ is an unbiased estimator when $I=1$ because with a single observation  $s_k s_n$ equals $s_{kn}$.}
As $I\to\infty$, the bias term goes to $\left[p_k p_n (1- \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn})\right]^2$
and the variance term goes to zero.

Which estimator has a smaller expected squared error depends on the contrast between
the variance of the calibrated-shares procedure (governed by sample size $I$) and the bias of the covariates-based specification (governed by unobserved commuting cost $(1- \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn})^2$).
The variance of the covariates-based specification is unimportant because
$\mathrm{var}(s_k s_n)$ is orders of magnitude smaller than $\mathrm{var}(s_{kn})$
(see footnote~\ref{footnote:variance:magnitudes}).
The relevant comparison is that the squared bias of the covariates-based specification is smaller than the variance of the frequency estimator if
\begin{align*}
\left[\mathbb{E}(s_k s_n - p_{kn})\right]^2 
&\leq 
\mathrm{var}(s_{kn})
\\
\iff
\left[\frac{I-1}{I} p_k p_n (1- \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn})\right]^2
&\leq
\frac{1}{I} \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}p_k p_n (1-\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}p_k p_n )
\\
\iff
\frac{(I-1)^2}{I} p_k p_n
&\leq
\frac{\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}}{\left(1- \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}\right)^2} (1 - \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} p_k p_n)
.\end{align*}
As $I$ becomes arbitrarily large, the left side becomes arbitrarily large and the inequality is necessarily false when $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} \neq 1$.
For a given $I$, as $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}$ approaches $1$, the right side becomes arbitrarily large and the inequality is necessarily true.
Denoting $\mathfrak{d}_{kn} \equiv \frac{(I-1)^2}{I} p_k p_n$,
the roots of the quadratic equation associated with the right side of the inequality are
$\frac{1 + 2 \mathfrak{d}_{kn}}{2 p_k p_n + 2 \mathfrak{d}_{kn}} \pm \frac{\sqrt{1+4\mathfrak{d}_{kn}(1 - p_k p_n)}}{2 p_k p_n + 2 \mathfrak{d}_{kn}}$,
and
the inequality above is true when $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}$ is close enough to one to lie between these roots.%
\footnote{
    When the right side equals $\mathfrak{d}_{kn}$,
    the associated quadratic equation is 
    $0 = (\mathfrak{d}_{kn} + p_k p_n) \left(\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}\right)^2 - (1 + 2\mathfrak{d}_{kn}) \lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn} + \mathfrak{d}_{kn}$.
}
Recall that the $p_k$- and $p_n$-weighted mean values of $\lambda^{-\epsilon}_{kn}$ equal one,
so its distribution must have substantial variance to make the inequality false for many $kn$ pairs.%
\footnote{
    For example, consider the NYC setting in which
    $I \approx 2.5 \times 10^6$, the average residential tract has $p_k \approx 0.5 \times 10^{-3}$,
    and the workplace tract containing 200 Fifth Avenue has $p_n \approx 0.5 \times 10^{-2}$,
    so that $\mathfrak{d}_{kn}$ is about $6.25$.
    When $\mathfrak{d}_{kn} = 6.25$, the inequality is true when
    $\epsilon \ln \lambda_{kn} \in [-0.397,0.397]$.
    For a log-normal distribution with unit mean,
    $\ln x \stackrel{\text{iid}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(\frac{-\sigma^2}{2},\sigma^2)$,
    to have half of its draws lie outside that interval,
    $\ln x \not\in [-0.397,0.397]$,
    its standard deviation must exceed approximately $0.56$.
    For comparison, in our setting with NYC tracts, the observable $\epsilon \ln \bar{\delta}_{kn}$ has a standard deviation of \input{../input/nyc2010_stddev_epslndelta.tex}\unskip.
}

These results describe the expected squared error for a given residence-workplace pair $kn$.
The Monte Carlo simulations of Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:MonteCarlo} and event studies of Section~\ref{sec:ctflgranular:eventstudies}
examine errors in predicted changes in the number of commuters to a specific workplace of interest.
There, we report the mean squared error for each simulation (each event),
which is the average of the squared errors over all residences paired with the workplace of interest.

\subsection{Welfare changes in the continuum model}\label{sec:appendix:theory:welfare}
Recall from equation~\eqref{eqn:utility} that the indirect utility of an individual $i$ residing in $k$ and working in $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
  U_{kn}^i = \epsilon \log \left( \frac{w_n}{r_k^\alpha P^{1-\alpha} \delta_{kn}} \right) + \nu_{kn}^i.
\end{equation*}
Following equation~(3.10) in \cite{Train:2009},
one can show that the expected utility $\bar{U}$ of each (ex ante identical) worker,
each of whom selects her utility-maximizing residence-workplace pair, is
\begin{equation} \label{eqn:welfare:expectedutility}
  \bar{U} = \mathbb{E}_{\nu^i} \left[ U_{kn}^i | U_{kn}^i \geq U_{k'n'}^i, \ \forall k',n' \right] = \log \left( \sum_{k',n'} w_{n'}^\epsilon \left( r_{k'}^\alpha P^{1-\alpha} \delta_{k'n'} \right)^{-\epsilon} \right) + c \quad \forall k,n,
\end{equation}
where $c$ is a constant.
Note that $c$ is unknown since the level of utility cannot be measured.
Denote $C_{kn} = w_n / \left( r_k^{\alpha} P^{1-\alpha} \bar{\delta}_{kn} \right)$
as the real consumption for individuals residing in $k$ and working in $n$.
We can rewrite equation~\eqref{eqn:welfare:expectedutility} in terms of real consumption:
\begin{equation*}
  \bar{U} = \log \left( \sum_{k,n} C_{kn}^\epsilon \lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} \right) + c,
\end{equation*}
where we use the decomposition of commuting costs $\delta_{kn}$ into time $\bar{\delta}_{kn}$
and disutility $\lambda_{kn}$ with $\delta_{kn} = \bar{\delta}_{kn} \lambda_{kn}$.

Now, consider a counterfactual equilibrium.
Recall that the counterfactual-to-baseline ratio of a variable $x>0$ is denoted by $\hat{x}\equiv \frac{x'}{x}$.
The expected utility in the counterfactual equilibrium is $\bar{U}' = \log \left( \sum_{k,n} (C_{kn}')^\epsilon (\lambda_{kn}')^{-\epsilon} \right) + c$. 
We define $1+\psi$ as the equivalent variation in consumption such that
\begin{equation*}
  \bar{U}' = \log \left( \sum_{k,n} (C_{kn}')^\epsilon (\lambda_{kn}')^{-\epsilon} \right) + c = \log \left( \sum_{k,n} \left[ (1+\psi) C_{kn} \right]^\epsilon \lambda_{kn}^{-\epsilon} \right) + c = \epsilon \log(1+\psi) + \bar{U}.
\end{equation*}
In other words, the equivalent variation in consumption is defined such that
multiplying the real consumption of individuals in all residence-workplace pairs by $1+\psi$
in the baseline equilibrium (holding prices and other allocations fixed) would generate the same welfare outcome
as the counterfactual equilibrium with new prices and allocations. 
Re-arranging the equation above yields an expression for $1+\psi$ in terms of welfare difference $\bar{U}' - \bar{U}$:
\begin{equation*}
  1+\psi = \exp \left( \frac{1}{\epsilon} \left[ \bar{U}' - \bar{U} \right] \right),
\end{equation*}
which is our measure of equivalent variation.

In practice, $1+\psi$ can be calculated from changes in wages ($\hat{w}_n$), rents ($\hat{r}_k$), and price index ($\hat{P}$),
given exogenous changes in productivities ($\hat{A}_n$), land endowments ($\hat{T}_k$), and commuting costs ($\hat{\delta}_{kn} = \hat{\bar{\delta}}_{kn} \hat{\lambda}_{kn}$), 
as well as initial wages ($w_n$) and commuting flows ($\ell_{kn}$).
One can show that
\begin{equation} \label{eqn:welfare:equivalentvariation}
  1+\psi = \left( \sum_{k,n} \frac{\ell_{kn}}{L} \hat{w}_n^\epsilon \left( \hat{r}_k^\alpha \hat{P}^{1-\alpha} \hat{\delta}_{kn} \right)^{-\epsilon} \right)^{\frac{1}{\epsilon}},
\end{equation}
where $\hat{w}_n$, $\hat{r}_k$, and $\hat{P}$ can be solved from equations~\eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:wages}, \eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:rents}, and \eqref{eqn:exacthatalgebra:laborallocation}
(with detailed derivations described in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix:theory:exacthatalgebra}).

\subsection{The trade equilibrium is unique}\label{sec:appendix:theory:finite:tradeeqlbmunique}

Recall that the trade equilibrium takes the labor allocation as given.
Consequently,
we just need to solve for wage and land rent vectors that satisfy
equations~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing} and~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing}
given arbitrary labor allocation $\ell_{kn}$.
Given a labor allocation and vector of wages,
equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing} determines a unique vector of land rents,
so we can focus on solving for a unique vector of relative wages.
Recall $L_n \equiv \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}$
is total labor supply in location $n$.
Substituting $L_n$ and $P = \left(\sum_n (w_n/A_n)^{1-\sigma}\right)^{1/(1-\sigma)}$
into equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing} yields
\begin{equation*}
A_n L_n 
=
\frac{(w_n / A_n)^{-\sigma}}{\sum_{n'} (w_{n'}/A_{n'})^{1-\sigma}} Y
\quad
\forall n.
\end{equation*}
Note that this equation is homogeneous of degree zero in the wage vector.
Selecting $w_1=1$ as the numeraire
and
writing the expression in terms of relative wages yields
\begin{equation*}
w_n
=
\left(\frac{A_n}{A_1}\right)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
\left(\frac{L_n}{L_1}\right)^{-1/\sigma}.
\end{equation*}
This defines a unique vector of relative wages.
Plugging those wages into equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing}
yields a unique vector of land rents.

\subsection{Continuum model as limiting case of the model with a finite number of individuals}\label{sec:appendix:theorycont}

We derive the result that the equilibrium of the model with a finite number of individuals coincides with the equilibrium of the continuum model as the number of individuals becomes infinite, $I \to \infty$.
Note that aggregate labor supply $L$ is fixed, as each individual supplies $L/I$ units of labor.
The key step is to show that equation~\eqref{eqn:cont_laborallocation} holds as $I \to \infty$.
Conditional on the labor allocation $\{\ell_{kn}\}$,
the number of individuals plays no role in the trade equilibrium,
which will coincide with that of a continuum model.

Definition~\ref{def:finitecommutingequilibrium} says that the share of labor supplied by residents of $k$ who work in $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\ell_{kn}}{L} = \frac{1}{I} \sum_{i=1}^I \textbf{1}\left\lbrace \tilde{U}^i_{kn}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I) >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I), \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)  \right\rbrace.
\end{equation*}
Since the \idiosyncraticphrase s are drawn from an independent and identically distributed random variable, the law of large numbers implies that
$\frac{1}{I} \sum_{i=1}^I \textbf{1}\left\lbrace \tilde{U}^i_{kn}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I) >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I), \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)  \right\rbrace$ converges to the mean of a binary random variable that is equal to one with probability  
$\Pr(\tilde{U}^i_{kn} >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}, \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n))$. Thus, 
$$\mathbb{E}\left(\textbf{1}\left\lbrace \tilde{U}^i_{kn} >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}, \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)  \right\rbrace\right)
=
\Pr\left(\tilde{U}^i_{kn} >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}, \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)\right).
$$ 
Using the probability in equation~\eqref{eq:gran_loglikelihood},
\begin{align*}
\lim_{I\to\infty}
\frac{1}{I} \sum_{i=1}^I \textbf{1}\left\lbrace \tilde{U}^i_{kn}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I) >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^I), \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)  \right\rbrace 
&=
\mathbb{E}\left(\textbf{1}\left\lbrace \tilde{U}^i_{kn} >  \tilde{U}^i_{k'n'}, \forall (k',n')  \neq (k,n)  \right\rbrace\right)
\\ 
&=
\frac{\tilde{w}^\epsilon_n (\tilde{r}^\alpha_k \delta_{kn})^{-\epsilon} }{\sum_{k',n'}\tilde{w}^\epsilon_{n'} (\tilde{r}^\alpha_{k'} \delta_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}
.\end{align*}
As a result, as $I \rightarrow \infty$,
$
\frac{\ell_{kn}}{L} \rightarrow \frac{\tilde{w}^\epsilon_n (\tilde{r}^\alpha_k \delta_{kn})^{-\epsilon} }{\sum_{k',n'}\tilde{w}^\epsilon_{n'} (\tilde{r}^\alpha_{k'} \delta_{k'n'})^{-\epsilon}}.
$
Since the continuum-case rational expectations are the wages and land rents that clear markets for this labor allocation,
the market-clearing prices of the model with a finite number of individuals converge to those values.

\subsection{Uncertainty about counterfactual changes due to idiosyncrasies: Analytical example \label{sec:appendix:theory:uncertainty}}

To analytically characterize the uncertainty in counterfactual changes caused by individuals' \idiosyncraticphrase s,
consider an economy with one residential location and two workplaces.
We compute the change in quantities associated with a change in economic primitives from $\Upsilon$ to $\Upsilon'$ 
for a given realization of \idiosyncraticphrase s $\boldsymbol \nu^{I}$.
An individual's utility from choosing workplace $n$ is
$\tilde{U}^{i}_{kn} = \tilde{U}_{kn} + \nu^{i}_{kn}$
in the baseline equilibrium
and
$\tilde{U}^{'i}_{kn} = \tilde{U}^{'}_{kn} + \nu^{i}_{kn}$
in the counterfactual equilibrium.
Because there is only one residential location, we suppress the $k$ subscript for convenience (e.g., denote $\tilde{U}_{k1}$ by $\tilde{U}_{1}$).

Suppose the counterfactual change improves the attractiveness of workplace $n=1$ relative to $n=2$:
$\tilde{U}'_{1} - \tilde{U}'_{2} > \tilde{U}_{1} - \tilde{U}_{2}$.
Individuals with $\nu^{i}_{1} - \nu^{i}_{2} < \tilde{U}'_{2} - \tilde{U}'_{1} $ choose workplace 2 in both the baseline and counterfactual equilibria.
Individuals with $\nu^{i}_{1} - \nu^{i}_{2} > \tilde{U}_{2} - \tilde{U}_{1} $ choose workplace 1 in both the baseline and counterfactual equilibria.
Those individuals who drew idiosyncratic shocks such that 
$
\nu^{i}_{1} - \nu^{i}_{2} \in \left[\tilde{U}'_{2} - \tilde{U}'_{1},\tilde{U}_{2} - \tilde{U}_{1}\right]
$
will change their workplace from $n=2$ in the baseline to $n=1$ in the counterfactual.
This interval is smaller if the counterfactual change is smaller.

The unconditional probability of leaving workplace 2 is
$
p
\equiv
\Pr\left(\tilde{U}'_{2} - \tilde{U}'_{1} < \nu^{i}_{1} - \nu^{i}_{2} < \tilde{U}_{2} - \tilde{U}_{1}\right)
=
F\left(\tilde{U}_{2} - \tilde{U}_{1}\right) - F\left(\tilde{U}'_{2} - \tilde{U}'_{1}\right)
$
where $F()$ denotes the cumulative distribution function of the logistic distribution.
This probability equals the share of switchers in the continuum model.
In the model with a finite number of individuals,
the unconditional number of switchers follows a binomial distribution with
$I$ independent outcomes and probability $p$ of switching.
The expected share of the population that switches is $p$.
The variance of the share that switches is $\frac{p(1-p)}{I}$.
With more independent draws, there is less uncertainty about the share who switch.

\subsection{Model extensions}\label{sec:appendix:theory:extensions}

\subsubsection{Trade costs}

Relative to the model in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel},
we now assume that goods trade is subject to iceberg trade costs:
delivering a unit of the location-$n$ variety to location $k$ requires producing $\tau_{nk} \geq 1$ units in $n$.
Individuals consume differentiated goods at their residences,
and the price of location $n$'s output for consumers in location $k$ is $\tau_{nk} w_n/A_n$.
Thus,
the price index in location $k$ is
$r_k^\alpha P_k^{1-\alpha}$,
where 
the local CES price index for goods is
$P_k = \left[\sum_{n} \left(\tau_{nk} w_n/A_n\right)^{1-\sigma}\right]^{1/(1-\sigma)}$.

In this environment, the equivalent of equation~\eqref{eqn:utility:beliefs} is such that,
based on the beliefs $\{\tilde{w}_{n}\}$ and $\{\tilde{r}_{k}\}$, each worker chooses the residential location and the work location that maximize expected utility,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:app_tc_util}
	\tilde{U}_{kn}^{i} = \underbrace{\epsilon \ln\left(\frac{\tilde{w}_n}{\tilde{r}_{k}^{\alpha} \tilde{P}_k^{1-\alpha} \delta_{kn}}\right)}_{\equiv \tilde{U}_{kn}} + \nu_{kn}^i
	,
\end{equation}
where $\tilde{P}_k = \left[\sum_{n} \left(\tau_{nk} \tilde{w}_n/A_n\right)^{1-\sigma}\right]^{1/(1-\sigma)}$.

With trade costs, the goods-market-clearing condition, which is the equivalent of equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing}, equates quantity supplied and quantity demanded:
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:tradecosts}
	A_n \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\delta_{kn}}
	=
	\left({w_n}/{A_n}\right)^{-\sigma} \sum_{k} \left[\left(\frac{\tau_{nk}}{P_{k}}\right)^{1-\sigma} \left(\sum_{n'} y_{kn'}\right)\right]
	.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing}, which clears the land market, is unchanged by the introduction of trade costs.

The definition of a trade equilibrium is akin to Definition~\ref{def:tradeequilibrium},
with equations~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:tradecosts} and \eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing} 
serving as the relevant market-clearing conditions.
The definition of a commuting equilibrium with finitely many individuals is akin to Definition~\ref{def:finitecommutingequilibrium} with $\{\tau_{kn}\}$ added to the list of economic primitives and $\tilde{U}_{kn}^{i}$ being defined in equation~\eqref{eq:app_tc_util}.



\subsubsection{Production uses land}

Relative to the model in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel},
we now assume that production of goods uses a Cobb-Douglas combination of labor and land inputs.
In particular, the quantity of output in workplace location $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
    q_n = A_n \left(\frac{L_n}{1-\beta}\right)^{1-\beta} \left(\frac{T_n^P}{\beta}\right)^{\beta} 
,\end{equation*}
where $L_n$ is labor and $T_n^P$ is land used in production.
Therefore, the unit cost of production in location $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
    c_n = \frac{1}{A_n} w_n^{1-\beta} r_n^{\beta}
\end{equation*}
and the CES price index is
$P = \left(\sum_n c_n^{1-\sigma}\right)^{1/(1-\sigma)}$.

Labor supplied in location $n$ is given by $L_n = \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\delta_{kn}}$.
From the first-order condition, land demanded for use in production in location $n$ can be expressed as
\begin{equation*}
    T_n^P = \frac{\beta}{1-\beta} \left(\frac{w_n}{r_n}\right) \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}.
\end{equation*}
Substituting this expression into the production technology,
output in location $n$ is
\begin{equation*}
    q_n = \frac{1}{1-\beta} A_n \left(\frac{w_n}{r_n}\right)^{\beta} \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}.
\end{equation*}

As in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel},
each individual devotes $1{-}\alpha$ of their expenditure to differentiated goods
and $\alpha$ of their expenditure to land,
while immobile landlords spend all of their income on differentiated goods,
such that total expenditure on differentiated goods equals aggregate income.
With land used in production, however, aggregate income now includes the income
that accrues to each factor.
Total income that accrues to labor is $\sum_{n} w_{n} L_{n}$.
Total income that accrues to land used in production equals
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{n'} r_{n'} T_{n'}^P
    =
    \frac{\beta}{1-\beta} \sum_{n'} w_{n'} \sum_{k'} \frac{\ell_{k'n'}}{\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}}
    =
    \frac{\beta}{1-\beta} \sum_{n'} w_{n'} L_{n'}
    .
\end{equation*}

The demand for each differentiated good stemming from CES preferences means that
demand is proportional to aggregate income.
Goods market clearing requires that quantity demanded equals quantity supplied in each location $n$:
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{1}{1-\beta} A_n \left(\frac{w_n}{r_n}\right)^{\beta} \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}
    =
    \frac{c_n^{-\sigma}}{P^{1-\sigma}} \left[\left(\frac{1}{1-\beta}\right) \sum_{n'} w_{n'} \sum_{k'} \frac{\ell_{k'n'}}{\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}} \right].
\end{equation*}
Writing the left-hand side in terms of the unit cost and simplifying yields
\begin{equation}
	\label{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:landlabor}
    w_n \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}}
    =
    \frac{c_n^{1-\sigma}}{P^{1-\sigma}} \left[\sum_{n'} w_{n'} \sum_{k'} \frac{\ell_{k'n'}}{\bar{\delta}_{k'n'}} \right].
\end{equation}

Residents spend a fraction $\alpha$ of their income on land, so their demand for land,
denoted $T_n^R$, is given by
\begin{equation*}
    T_n^R = \alpha \sum_k \frac{\ell_{nn'}}{\bar{\delta}_{nn'}} \frac{w_{n'}}{r_n} = \frac{\alpha}{r_{n}} \sum_{n'} y_{nn'}
.\end{equation*}
Clearing the land market means
equating the fixed land supply $T_n$ to the sum of the quantities of land demanded by producers and residents:
\begin{equation}
	\label{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing:landlabor}
    T_n = T_n^P + T_n^R
    = \frac{1}{r_n} \left[\frac{\beta}{1-\beta} \sum_k \frac{\ell_{kn}}{\bar{\delta}_{kn}} w_n
    + \alpha \sum_{n'} \frac{\ell_{nn'}}{\bar{\delta}_{nn'}} w_{n'} \right].
\end{equation}

The definition of a trade equilibrium is akin to Definition~\ref{def:tradeequilibrium},
with equations~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:landlabor} and \eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing:landlabor}
serving as the relevant market-clearing conditions.
The definition of a commuting equilibrium with finitely many individuals is akin to Definition~\ref{def:finitecommutingequilibrium} with the Cobb-Douglas production parameter $\beta$ added to the list of economic primitives.

\subsubsection{Residential amenities}\label{sec:appendix:theory:amenities}

Relative to the model in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel},
we now assume that each location is endowed with a residential amenity $B_k$.
Individual $i$'s utility from residing in $k$ and working in $n$ is now 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:app_ra_util}
    \tilde{U}_{kn}^{i} = \epsilon \ln\left(\frac{B_k \tilde{w}_n}{\tilde{r}_k^{\alpha}P^{1-\alpha} \delta_{kn}}\right) + \nu_{kn}^i
    .
\end{equation}
Note that local amenities can depend on local residential density as in \cite{AllenArkolakis:2014}:
$B_k = \bar{B}_k \left( \sum_n \ell_{kn} \right)^{-\theta}$,
where $\bar{B}_k$ is the fundamental amenities in location $k$, $\sum_n \ell_{kn}$ is the total number of individuals residing in $k$, and $\theta\geq 0$ governs the strength of the congestion force.
When $\theta = 0$, local amenities are exogenous. 


The definition of a trade equilibrium in Definition~\ref{def:tradeequilibrium} remains unchanged.
The definition of a commuting equilibrium with finitely many individuals is akin to Definition~\ref{def:finitecommutingequilibrium} with the fundamental residential amenities $\{\bar{B}_k\}$ and $\theta$ added to the list of economic primitives
and $\tilde{U}_{kn}^{i} $ being defined in equation~\eqref{eq:app_ra_util}.

\subsubsection{Local increasing returns}
\label{sec:appendix:local_inc_returns_theory}

Relative to the model in Section~\ref{sec:finitemodel},
we now assume that production exhibits local external economies of scale.
In particular, in each location $n$ the linear production technology is $q_n = A_n L_n$,
where $L_n$ is the labor supply of workers working in location $n$
and $A_n \equiv \bar{A}_n L_n^{\eta}$.
Thus, the CES price index is 
$P = \left[\sum_{n} \left(w_n/(\bar{A}_n L_n^{\eta})\right)^{1-\sigma}\right]^{1/(1-\sigma)}$.

Since output in location $n$ is $\bar{A}_n \left(\sum_k \ell_{kn}/\bar{\delta}_{kn}\right)^{1+\eta}$,
equating quantity supplied and quantity demanded requires
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:localIR}
\bar{A}_n \left(\sum_k \ell_{kn}/\bar{\delta}_{kn}\right)^{1+\eta}
=
\frac{{w_n}^{-\sigma}\bar{A}_n^{\sigma} \left(\sum_k \ell_{kn}/\bar{\delta}_{kn}\right)^{\eta\sigma}}{P^{1-\sigma}} Y.
\end{equation}

The remaining results are unchanged after replacing equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing}
with equation~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:localIR}.
The definition of a trade equilibrium is akin to Definition~\ref{def:tradeequilibrium},
with equations~\eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:goodsmarketclearing:localIR} and \eqref{eqn:tradeeqlbm:landsmarketclearing} 
serving as the relevant market-clearing conditions.
The definition of a commuting equilibrium with finitely many individuals is akin to Definition~\ref{def:finitecommutingequilibrium} with $\{\bar{A}_{n}\}$ and $\eta$ added to the list of economic primitives.

Local increasing returns enter the sufficient conditions for the existence and uniqueness of the equilibrium in the continuum model and thereby the uniqueness of the continuum-case rational expectations.
We assume that $\eta < \frac{1}{\sigma-1}$.
We define  $\psi = 1+\eta-\eta\sigma$.
Following the same steps as in Appendix \ref{sec:appendix:theory:continuumexistence},
the relevant matrix whose spectral radius we need to evaluate is
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbf{A} =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        0 & \frac{\psi(1+\epsilon)}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} & \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} \\
        \frac{\alpha \epsilon}{1 + \alpha \epsilon} & 0 & 0 \\
        0 & \frac{|\sigma-\psi|}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} & \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} \\
    \end{bmatrix}
.\end{equation*}
As in Appendix \ref{sec:appendix:theory:continuumexistence}, $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \geq 1$ and $\rho(\mathbf{A}) \leq 1$ if and only if $f_k(1) \geq 0$ for $k = 0,1,2$.  
\begin{align*}
  f(1) &=
  1 - \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} - \psi \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} \right)
  \left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{1+\alpha\epsilon}\right) \left(1 - \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon}\right) - 
  \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{1+\alpha\epsilon}\right) \left(\frac{|\sigma - \psi|}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon}\right) \\
  f_1(1) &= 3 - 2 \left( \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon}  \right) - 
  \psi \left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon} \right) \left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{1+\alpha\epsilon}\right) \\
  f_2(1) &= 6 - 2 \left( \frac{1+\epsilon}{\sigma + \psi\epsilon}  \right).
\end{align*}
Notice that $f_2(1) > f_1(1)$ under the parametric assumptions. 
We proceed by numerically finding the values of $\eta$ 
that satisfy $f(1) \geq 0$ and $f_1(1) \geq 0$ given values of $\alpha$, $\sigma$, and $\epsilon$.
Under the baseline parameter values ($\alpha=0.24,\sigma=4,\epsilon=7.986$),
$f(1) \geq 0$ and $f_1(1) \geq 0$ are satisfied when $\eta \leq 0.0029$.
The equilibrium exists and is unique up to scale when agglomeration forces are modest.

\subsection{Galton's fallacy for Poisson counts}\label{sec:appendix:theory:Galton}

When a process is serially uncorrelated or mean reverting,
changes will be negatively correlated with prior levels.
Consider an AR(1) process such that
\begin{equation*}
y_{t} = (1-\tilde{\rho}) \tilde{\mu} + \tilde{\rho} y_{t-1} + \epsilon_{t}
,\end{equation*}
where
$\tilde{\mu}$ is the unconditional mean of $y_t$,
$\tilde{\rho} \in [0,1)$ is its serial correlation,
and $\epsilon_{t}$ is iid white noise with mean zero and variance $\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}$.
If $\tilde{\rho}=0$, $y_{t}$ is independently and identical distributed.

This process can be rewritten by iterating and then taking the difference:
\begin{align*}
y_{t}
&=
(1-\tilde{\rho}) \tilde{\mu} + \tilde{\rho} y_{t-1} + \epsilon_{t}
\\
&=
\tilde{\mu} + \sum^{\infty}_{i=0}\tilde{\rho}^{i}\epsilon_{t-i}
\\
y_{t+1} - y_{t}
&= \epsilon_{t+1} + (\tilde{\rho}-1)\sum^{\infty}_{i=0}\tilde{\rho}^{i} \epsilon_{t-i}
.\end{align*}
As a result, the initial level and subsequent change are negatively correlated:
$
\text{Cov}\left(y_{t}, y_{t+1} - y_{t}\right) 
=
\frac{-1}{1+\tilde{\rho}}\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon} < 0
$.

We now consider commuter counts, which, unlike the AR(1) process above, must be integers.
Consider a Poisson process with an AR(1)-style mean:
\begin{align*}
y_{t+1}
&\sim
\text{Poisson}\left((1-\rho)\mu + \rho y_{t}\right) \\
y_{0}
&\sim
\text{Poisson}\left(\mu\right),
\end{align*}
where $\rho \in [0,1)$ is the serial correlation coefficient and $\rho = 0$ is the iid case.
We define the initial condition to define the unconditional mean.

The following identities are helpful in showing the relationship between a count and its subsequent change:
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb{E}[y_{t+1}] 
=
\mu
,\qquad
\mathbb{E}\left[y^{2}_{t}\right] 
=
\mu^{2} + \frac{\mu}{1-\rho^{2}}
,\qquad
\mathbb{E}[y_{t+1}y_{t}]
=
\mu^{2} + \frac{\rho\mu}{1-\rho^{2}}
.\end{equation*}
Using these, we can show that the covariance is negative:
\begin{equation*}
\text{Cov}\left(y_{t}, y_{t+1}-y_{t}\right)
= 
\text{Cov}\left(y_{t}, y_{t+1}\right) - \text{Var}\left(y_{t}\right)
=
\frac{\rho}{1-\rho^{2}}\mu - \frac{\mu}{1-\rho^{2}}
=
\frac{\rho-1}{1-\rho^{2}}\mu
=
\frac{-1}{1+\rho}\mu
<
0
.\end{equation*}
This matches the expression for the continuous-valued distributions above.
\clearpage
\section{Descriptive statistics and data sources}\label{sec:appendix:empirics}

Section~\ref{sec:DetroitMSP} presents descriptive statistics for Detroit and Minneapolis-St. Paul analogous to those for New York City presented in the main text.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:empirics:counties} examines commuting flows between counties.
Section~\ref{sec:impersistent} shows that commuting flows are often impersistent.
Section~\ref{sec:asym_zeros} shows that zero commuting flows are often asymmetric.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:empirics:ACSmigration} shows that small and zero flows are a large proportion of cells in the migration matrix and that these flows are impersistent over time.
Section~\ref{sec:appendix:estimation:OLSbias}  shows that estimates of workplace fixed effects are biased by dropping observations with zero commuters.
Section~\ref{sec:data_sources} describes the data sources we employ.

\subsection{County-level commuting flows}\label{sec:appendix:empirics:counties}

Even when studying larger geographic units,
many pairs of locations may have small flows.
Consider US counties, which vary greatly in population size.
\input{../input/counties_are_granular.tex}%
Almost half of the county pairs report zero commuters.
Among those reporting positive values,
Figure~\ref{fig:UScounties:histogram} shows that this distribution is skewed, so that many thousands of county pairs have small numbers of commuters.
Unlike census tracts, however, these small flows constitute a small share of the total number of commuters.
That is,
US counties with tens of thousands of residents
do not exhibit the patterns we described for granular settings,
but many county pairs have small commuting flows and show similar patterns.

\label{fig:UScounties:histogram}
